If they start giving me instructions, I’ll cut in with, “Oh, so you want me to do this,” before they’ve actually finished saying it. And then sometimes they’ll have to stop me and say, “No, that’s not what I meant,” or correct me because I guessed wrong. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be rude. It just comes out before I’ve really had a chance to hold it back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like doing things on the spur of the moment, outside of just speaking or interrupting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so, though for me it shows up a lot as not being able to wait my turn, if that counts as acting too fast. Like at work, if I’m waiting on the freight elevator with my cart, and it’s taking forever, I get really antsy. I’ll pace back and forth, keep hitting the button even though I know that doesn’t help, or start trying to figure out some other way to get moving before it’s really time.\n\nSame thing with the supply closet key. If somebody else has it, I have a hard time just standing there calmly waiting. I’ll be asking, “Are you almost done with that?” or hovering around, and I can tell I’m getting short with people even when they’re not doing anything wrong. Or if we’re waiting for someone to decide which room needs cleaning next, I’m already wanting to jump in and start somewhere just so I’m not stuck waiting.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to bulldoze people, but that waiting feeling makes me feel wound up and impatient, and I push to get things moving before it’s really my turn.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure on that one. I mean, I know I make mistakes like anyone does, especially if I’m tired or there’s a lot going on, but I don’t know that I’d say I’m constantly missing little details in my work.\n\nWith cleaning, a lot of it is pretty routine, so I can usually see if something’s obviously not done right — like if a trash can wasn’t emptied or a floor still looks dirty. But as far as “careless mistakes,” I guess I’d have to think about it more. Nobody’s really sat me down and said that’s a big problem with me, at least not that I can remember. So… I don’t know. That’s not the first thing that comes to mind for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I do have routines at work, so in that sense the job kind of organizes me. There are certain areas that have to be cleaned, trash has to go out, supplies have to be checked, that kind of thing. If I’m following the usual routine, I can usually get through it.\n\nTime management… I don’t know. I don’t think of myself as someone who’s always late or totally unable to plan, but I guess it depends what you mean. If the day gets changed around or someone asks me to do things in a different order, then I might have to stop and figure out what’s most important first. But I’m not sure I’d say organizing tasks is my main problem.\n\nAt home, too, I sort of do things as they come up. I don’t really sit down and make a big plan, but I get by. So I guess my answer is maybe sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as a major issue the way some other things are.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I don’t really think of myself that way, like getting so locked into something that I forget the whole world is there.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I’m doing a task, especially something repetitive, I can kind of get into the rhythm of it — like cleaning a stretch of floor or working through a set of rooms — but I wouldn’t say I lose track of everything around me. I still usually notice if somebody comes by or if I’m needed somewhere else. At least I think I do.\n\nMaybe if I’m watching something at home or looking at something on my phone, I might tune out a little, but I don’t know if that’s more than what most people do. Nobody’s really told me, “You were impossible to get through to,” or anything like that. So I guess my answer is… not really, or not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so absorbed in an activity that you forget about other things that are happening around you?\n\nPatient: I guess I’d say I’m not really sure, but not in a way that stands out to me. Like, I can get into doing something, sure — if I’m cleaning an area and I’ve got a pattern going, or if I’m at home watching something, I might be focused on that for a while. But “so absorbed” that I don’t know what’s happening around me… I don’t think that really happens much.\n\nI usually still have some awareness of people moving around or someone needing something. At work especially, you kind of have to keep one ear open because people might come through or ask for something, so I don’t think I disappear into a task like that. Maybe I zone in a little here and there, but I haven’t noticed myself forgetting everything else around me.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse ever caused problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I’d have to say yes, at least at work. I don’t love saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m just careless or trying to be difficult, but it has started causing problems.\n\nThe main thing is when we’re turning rooms over or when there are supplies to decide about — like what needs restocking first, what can wait, which rooms need to be done in what order. If instructions come at me quickly, or if there are a couple people talking, I’ll catch part of it and then move on like I understood the whole thing. Then later someone says, “No, that’s not what I told you,” or “Why did you do that first?” And I realize I missed a piece of what they said.\n\nIt can also create friction with coworkers, because I might jump in with what I think needs to happen or make a quick call about supplies before everyone’s agreed on it. In my mind I’m trying to keep things moving, but it comes across like I’m not listening or I’m pushing ahead. I’ve been corrected for it more than once.\n\nMy supervisor has complained about it too, not just casually. It got to the point where he suggested — or kind of pushed me toward — using the employee assistance program. That was embarrassing, honestly. I don’t think I’m causing huge disasters or anything, but it’s enough that people are noticing and saying something. At home or relationships, I’m less sure how to measure it, but at work, yeah, it has caused real problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting, and acting on impulse consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been the same all through my adult life, no. I mean, I’ve probably always been the kind of person who likes to keep moving and maybe talks or jumps in a little fast sometimes, but the part where it’s really causing problems — where people are correcting me and I’m making quick decisions before I’ve fully heard everything — that’s been more recent.\n\nIt’s been, let me think… about a year and five months now, roughly. It really started getting worse after my cleaning contract changed. Before that, the work was more steady and predictable, and I could kind of get into a routine. But once it switched to rotating hours and more event work, everything got more rushed and changeable. Different timing, different rooms, different priorities depending on what was going on that day.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had this exact level of trouble consistently my whole adult life. This worse stretch has been going on for about a year and five months, since that contract change.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse since childhood and continued to have them as an adult?\n\nPatient: Yeah… when you put it that way, I guess yes, there was some of that going back pretty far. I don’t know that I would’ve described it the same way when I was a kid, but I do remember in grade school getting in trouble for not staying in my seat. Like I’d pop up when I wasn’t supposed to, or start moving around before the teacher was done talking.\n\nAnd talking over people — yeah, I got corrected for that too. I can remember teachers saying I needed to wait my turn, or that I was jumping in before they finished giving directions. I’d think I knew what they were going to say and just rush ahead, and then sometimes I’d miss the rest of the instructions.\n\nMy mother used to say I was like I was “wound too tight,” even as a kid. That’s the phrase I remember. Not in a mean way exactly, but like I had this motor in me and she couldn’t get me to settle down. So yes, there’s a childhood version of it. I think the work problems now are more noticeable because there are consequences, but the pattern of being too quick, too restless, interrupting — that wasn’t brand new.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse consistently since childhood into your adult life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it’s not like I had the words for it then, but I remember pretty clearly getting in trouble back in grade school for not staying put. I’d pop out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d be halfway up before the teacher was even done explaining what we were supposed to do.\n\nAnd I did talk over the teacher sometimes. Not trying to be disrespectful, at least not how I remember it, but I’d think I knew where they were going with something and I’d jump in or start doing the assignment before the directions were finished. Then, of course, I’d miss some important part and have to be corrected.\n\nMy mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” even when I was a kid. That phrase stuck with me because she said it more than once — like I had too much motor in me and couldn’t settle myself down. So, yeah, I’d say it’s been there since childhood in some form, even if the way it shows up now as an adult is a little different.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life, beyond just the issues at work?\n\nPatient: Well… the biggest problems I can point to are still at work, if I’m being honest. That’s where it shows up most clearly and where people actually say something to me about it.\n\nIt’s been causing complaints and corrections, not just little annoyances. Like during room turnovers, when everything has to move fast, I’ll jump in before I’ve heard the whole instruction, or I’ll think I know what needs doing and start moving supplies or making a decision about what goes where. Then it turns out I missed part of what was said, or someone else had already planned it differently, and that creates friction. People get irritated because they feel like I’m not listening or I’m pushing ahead, and I can see why it comes across that way.\n\nThe supply decisions are another one. I’ll act too quickly — like decide what needs to be brought up or used without fully checking or waiting — and then my supervisor has to correct me. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be difficult. I’m trying to be useful and keep things moving, but I end up making it harder sometimes.\n\nIt got to the point where my supervisor actually pushed me toward the employee assistance program, which felt… pretty serious to me. So yes, it’s causing problems in daily life in the sense that it’s not just something I notice inside my own head. Other people are reacting to it, correcting me, complaining, and it’s affecting how I get through the day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F90.8", "F90.9"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I can remember teachers saying I needed to wait my turn, or that I was jumping in before they finished giving directions. I’d think I knew what they were going to say and just rush ahead, and then sometimes I’d miss the rest of the instructions.\n\nMy mother used to say I was like I was “wound too tight,” even as a kid. That’s the phrase I remember. Not in a mean way exactly, but like I had this motor in me and she couldn’t get me to settle down. So yes, there’s a childhood version of it. I think the work problems now are more noticeable because there are consequences, but the pattern of being too quick, too restless, interrupting — that wasn’t brand new.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse consistently since childhood into your adult life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it’s not like I had the words for it then, but I remember pretty clearly getting in trouble back in grade school for not staying put. I’d pop out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d be halfway up before the teacher was even done explaining what we were supposed to do.\n\nAnd I did talk over the teacher sometimes. Not trying to be disrespectful, at least not how I remember it, but I’d think I knew where they were going with something and I’d jump in or start doing the assignment before the directions were finished. Then, of course, I’d miss some important part and have to be corrected.\n\nMy mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” even when I was a kid. That phrase stuck with me because she said it more than once — like I had too much motor in me and couldn’t settle myself down. So, yeah, I’d say it’s been there since childhood in some form, even if the way it shows up now as an adult is a little different.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life, beyond just the issues at work?\n\nPatient: Well… the biggest problems I can point to are still at work, if I’m being honest. That’s where it shows up most clearly and where people actually say something to me about it.\n\nIt’s been causing complaints and corrections, not just little annoyances. Like during room turnovers, when everything has to move fast, I’ll jump in before I’ve heard the whole instruction, or I’ll think I know what needs doing and start moving supplies or making a decision about what goes where. Then it turns out I missed part of what was said, or someone else had already planned it differently, and that creates friction. People get irritated because they feel like I’m not listening or I’m pushing ahead, and I can see why it comes across that way.\n\nThe supply decisions are another one. I’ll act too quickly — like decide what needs to be brought up or used without fully checking or waiting — and then my supervisor has to correct me. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be difficult. I’m trying to be useful and keep things moving, but I end up making it harder sometimes.\n\nIt got to the point where my supervisor actually pushed me toward the employee assistance program, which felt… pretty serious to me. So yes, it’s causing problems in daily life in the sense that it’s not just something I notice inside my own head. Other people are reacting to it, correcting me, complaining, and it’s affecting how I get through the day.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse for at least 6 months?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Like at work, if I’m waiting on the freight elevator with my cart, and it’s taking forever, I get really antsy. I’ll pace back and forth, keep hitting the button even though I know that doesn’t help, or start trying to figure out some other way to get moving before it’s really time.\n\nSame thing with the supply closet key. If somebody else has it, I have a hard time just standing there calmly waiting. I’ll be asking, “Are you almost done with that?” or hovering around, and I can tell I’m getting short with people even when they’re not doing anything wrong. But as far as “careless mistakes,” I guess I’d have to think about it more. Nobody’s really sat me down and said that’s a big problem with me, at least not that I can remember. So… I don’t know. I don’t really think of myself that way, like getting so locked into something that I forget the whole world is there.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I’m doing a task, especially something repetitive, I can kind of get into the rhythm of it — like cleaning a stretch of floor or working through a set of rooms — but I wouldn’t say I lose track of everything around me. I still usually notice if somebody comes by or if I’m needed somewhere else. It just comes out before I’ve really had a chance to hold it back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like doing things on the spur of the moment, outside of just speaking or interrupting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so, though for me it shows up a lot as not being able to wait my turn, if that counts as acting too fast. Like at work, if I’m waiting on the freight elevator with my cart, and it’s taking forever, I get really antsy. I’ll pace back and forth, keep hitting the button even though I know that doesn’t help, or start trying to figure out some other way to get moving before it’s really time.\n\nSame thing with the supply closet key. If somebody else has it, I have a hard time just standing there calmly waiting. I’ll be asking, “Are you almost done with that?” or hovering around, and I can tell I’m getting short with people even when they’re not doing anything wrong. Or if we’re waiting for someone to decide which room needs cleaning next, I’m already wanting to jump in and start somewhere just so I’m not stuck waiting.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to bulldoze people, but that waiting feeling makes me feel wound up and impatient, and I push to get things moving before it’s really my turn.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure on that one. I mean, I know I make mistakes like anyone does, especially if I’m tired or there’s a lot going on, but I don’t know that I’d say I’m constantly missing little details in my work.\n\nWith cleaning, a lot of it is pretty routine, so I can usually see if something’s obviously not done right — like if a trash can wasn’t emptied or a floor still looks dirty. But as far as “careless mistakes,” I guess I’d have to think about it more. Nobody’s really sat me down and said that’s a big problem with me, at least not that I can remember. So… I don’t know. That’s not the first thing that comes to mind for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I do have routines at work, so in that sense the job kind of organizes me. There are certain areas that have to be cleaned, trash has to go out, supplies have to be checked, that kind of thing. If I’m following the usual routine, I can usually get through it.\n\nTime management… I don’t know. I don’t think of myself as someone who’s always late or totally unable to plan, but I guess it depends what you mean. If the day gets changed around or someone asks me to do things in a different order, then I might have to stop and figure out what’s most important first. But I’m not sure I’d say organizing tasks is my main problem.\n\nAt home, too, I sort of do things as they come up. I don’t really sit down and make a big plan, but I get by. So I guess my answer is maybe sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as a major issue the way some other things are.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I don’t really think of myself that way, like getting so locked into something that I forget the whole world is there.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I’m doing a task, especially something repetitive, I can kind of get into the rhythm of it — like cleaning a stretch of floor or working through a set of rooms — but I wouldn’t say I lose track of everything around me. I still usually notice if somebody comes by or if I’m needed somewhere else. At least I think I do.\n\nMaybe if I’m watching something at home or looking at something on my phone, I might tune out a little, but I don’t know if that’s more than what most people do. Nobody’s really told me, “You were impossible to get through to,” or anything like that. So I guess my answer is… not really, or not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so absorbed in an activity that you forget about other things that are happening around you?\n\nPatient: I guess I’d say I’m not really sure, but not in a way that stands out to me. Like, I can get into doing something, sure — if I’m cleaning an area and I’ve got a pattern going, or if I’m at home watching something, I might be focused on that for a while. But “so absorbed” that I don’t know what’s happening around me… I don’t think that really happens much.\n\nI usually still have some awareness of people moving around or someone needing something. At work especially, you kind of have to keep one ear open because people might come through or ask for something, so I don’t think I disappear into a task like that. Maybe I zone in a little here and there, but I haven’t noticed myself forgetting everything else around me.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse ever caused problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I’d have to say yes, at least at work. I don’t love saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m just careless or trying to be difficult, but it has started causing problems.\n\nThe main thing is when we’re turning rooms over or when there are supplies to decide about — like what needs restocking first, what can wait, which rooms need to be done in what order. If instructions come at me quickly, or if there are a couple people talking, I’ll catch part of it and then move on like I understood the whole thing. Then later someone says, “No, that’s not what I told you,” or “Why did you do that first?” And I realize I missed a piece of what they said.\n\nIt can also create friction with coworkers, because I might jump in with what I think needs to happen or make a quick call about supplies before everyone’s agreed on it. In my mind I’m trying to keep things moving, but it comes across like I’m not listening or I’m pushing ahead. I’ve been corrected for it more than once.\n\nMy supervisor has complained about it too, not just casually. It got to the point where he suggested — or kind of pushed me toward — using the employee assistance program. That was embarrassing, honestly. I don’t think I’m causing huge disasters or anything, but it’s enough that people are noticing and saying something. At home or relationships, I’m less sure how to measure it, but at work, yeah, it has caused real problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting, and acting on impulse consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been the same all through my adult life, no. I mean, I’ve probably always been the kind of person who likes to keep moving and maybe talks or jumps in a little fast sometimes, but the part where it’s really causing problems — where people are correcting me and I’m making quick decisions before I’ve fully heard everything — that’s been more recent.\n\nIt’s been, let me think… about a year and five months now, roughly. It really started getting worse after my cleaning contract changed. Before that, the work was more steady and predictable, and I could kind of get into a routine. But once it switched to rotating hours and more event work, everything got more rushed and changeable. Different timing, different rooms, different priorities depending on what was going on that day.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had this exact level of trouble consistently my whole adult life. This worse stretch has been going on for about a year and five months, since that contract change.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse since childhood and continued to have them as an adult?\n\nPatient: Yeah… when you put it that way, I guess yes, there was some of that going back pretty far. I don’t know that I would’ve described it the same way when I was a kid, but I do remember in grade school getting in trouble for not staying in my seat. Like I’d pop up when I wasn’t supposed to, or start moving around before the teacher was done talking.\n\nAnd talking over people — yeah, I got corrected for that too. I can remember teachers saying I needed to wait my turn, or that I was jumping in before they finished giving directions. I’d think I knew what they were going to say and just rush ahead, and then sometimes I’d miss the rest of the instructions.\n\nMy mother used to say I was like I was “wound too tight,” even as a kid. That’s the phrase I remember. Not in a mean way exactly, but like I had this motor in me and she couldn’t get me to settle down. So yes, there’s a childhood version of it. I think the work problems now are more noticeable because there are consequences, but the pattern of being too quick, too restless, interrupting — that wasn’t brand new.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse consistently since childhood into your adult life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it’s not like I had the words for it then, but I remember pretty clearly getting in trouble back in grade school for not staying put. I’d pop out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d be halfway up before the teacher was even done explaining what we were supposed to do.\n\nAnd I did talk over the teacher sometimes. Not trying to be disrespectful, at least not how I remember it, but I’d think I knew where they were going with something and I’d jump in or start doing the assignment before the directions were finished. Then, of course, I’d miss some important part and have to be corrected.\n\nMy mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” even when I was a kid. That phrase stuck with me because she said it more than once — like I had too much motor in me and couldn’t settle myself down. So, yeah, I’d say it’s been there since childhood in some form, even if the way it shows up now as an adult is a little different.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life, beyond just the issues at work?\n\nPatient: Well… the biggest problems I can point to are still at work, if I’m being honest. That’s where it shows up most clearly and where people actually say something to me about it.\n\nIt’s been causing complaints and corrections, not just little annoyances. Like during room turnovers, when everything has to move fast, I’ll jump in before I’ve heard the whole instruction, or I’ll think I know what needs doing and start moving supplies or making a decision about what goes where. Then it turns out I missed part of what was said, or someone else had already planned it differently, and that creates friction. People get irritated because they feel like I’m not listening or I’m pushing ahead, and I can see why it comes across that way.\n\nThe supply decisions are another one. I’ll act too quickly — like decide what needs to be brought up or used without fully checking or waiting — and then my supervisor has to correct me. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be difficult. I’m trying to be useful and keep things moving, but I end up making it harder sometimes.\n\nIt got to the point where my supervisor actually pushed me toward the employee assistance program, which felt… pretty serious to me. So yes, it’s causing problems in daily life in the sense that it’s not just something I notice inside my own head. Other people are reacting to it, correcting me, complaining, and it’s affecting how I get through the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes — definitely more than six months. The worse stretch, the part where it’s been really noticeable and causing problems, has been going on for about a year and five months now.\n\nIt really seemed to kick up after my cleaning contract changed. My hours started rotating more, and there was more event work involved, so the routine wasn’t as steady as it used to be. Since then, the sitting still, jumping in too fast, interrupting or acting before I’ve fully taken things in — that’s been pretty consistent. It’s not like it was just a bad week or a short period. It’s been over a year, closer to a year and a half.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yes, over the past year it’s been pretty consistent. I’d actually put it a little longer than that — about a year and five months, give or take.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess the main thing is I can’t seem to sit still when I’m supposed to. It’s like there’s this motor humming under my skin, if that makes sense. Like in staff meetings at work, or sometimes at church when they’re doing event briefings and everybody’s sitting there listening, I’m trying to stay put but my body just wants to move. I’ll shift around in the chair, tap my feet, stand up if I can get away with it, or I’ll find some excuse like needing to check something or grab water just so I can move around a little. It’s uncomfortable, like I’m crawling out of my own skin when I have to just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I remember getting in trouble for popping up out of my seat when we were supposed to be doing quiet work, or blurting things out and talking over the teacher before I even realized I was doing it. And if the teacher was giving directions, I’d be halfway into the assignment before she was done explaining, then I’d have to be corrected because I’d missed something important.\n\nMy mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” like I had too much energy packed into me. At the time I just thought I was being a normal kid, but looking back, yeah, sitting still was a real problem for me even then.\n\nDoctor: Did you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finished speaking when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did, and honestly I still do that now. Back in school I would jump in before the teacher finished the question, like my mouth would just go ahead of me. I’d think I knew where they were going, so I’d answer too fast, and sometimes I was wrong because I hadn’t let them finish.\n\nThat still happens with my supervisor or with people at church. If they start giving me instructions, I’ll cut in with, “Oh, so you want me to do this,” before they’ve actually finished saying it. And then sometimes they’ll have to stop me and say, “No, that’s not what I meant,” or correct me because I guessed wrong. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be rude. It just comes out before I’ve really had a chance to hold it back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like doing things on the spur of the moment, outside of just speaking or interrupting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so, though for me it shows up a lot as not being able to wait my turn, if that counts as acting too fast. Like at work, if I’m waiting on the freight elevator with my cart, and it’s taking forever, I get really antsy. I’ll pace back and forth, keep hitting the button even though I know that doesn’t help, or start trying to figure out some other way to get moving before it’s really time.\n\nSame thing with the supply closet key. If somebody else has it, I have a hard time just standing there calmly waiting. I’ll be asking, “Are you almost done with that?” or hovering around, and I can tell I’m getting short with people even when they’re not doing anything wrong. Or if we’re waiting for someone to decide which room needs cleaning next, I’m already wanting to jump in and start somewhere just so I’m not stuck waiting.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to bulldoze people, but that waiting feeling makes me feel wound up and impatient, and I push to get things moving before it’s really my turn.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure on that one. I mean, I know I make mistakes like anyone does, especially if I’m tired or there’s a lot going on, but I don’t know that I’d say I’m constantly missing little details in my work.\n\nWith cleaning, a lot of it is pretty routine, so I can usually see if something’s obviously not done right — like if a trash can wasn’t emptied or a floor still looks dirty. But as far as “careless mistakes,” I guess I’d have to think about it more. Nobody’s really sat me down and said that’s a big problem with me, at least not that I can remember. So… I don’t know. That’s not the first thing that comes to mind for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I do have routines at work, so in that sense the job kind of organizes me. There are certain areas that have to be cleaned, trash has to go out, supplies have to be checked, that kind of thing. If I’m following the usual routine, I can usually get through it.\n\nTime management… I don’t know. I don’t think of myself as someone who’s always late or totally unable to plan, but I guess it depends what you mean. If the day gets changed around or someone asks me to do things in a different order, then I might have to stop and figure out what’s most important first. But I’m not sure I’d say organizing tasks is my main problem.\n\nAt home, too, I sort of do things as they come up. I don’t really sit down and make a big plan, but I get by. So I guess my answer is maybe sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as a major issue the way some other things are.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I don’t really think of myself that way, like getting so locked into something that I forget the whole world is there.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I’m doing a task, especially something repetitive, I can kind of get into the rhythm of it — like cleaning a stretch of floor or working through a set of rooms — but I wouldn’t say I lose track of everything around me. I still usually notice if somebody comes by or if I’m needed somewhere else. At least I think I do.\n\nMaybe if I’m watching something at home or looking at something on my phone, I might tune out a little, but I don’t know if that’s more than what most people do. Nobody’s really told me, “You were impossible to get through to,” or anything like that. So I guess my answer is… not really, or not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so absorbed in an activity that you forget about other things that are happening around you?\n\nPatient: I guess I’d say I’m not really sure, but not in a way that stands out to me. Like, I can get into doing something, sure — if I’m cleaning an area and I’ve got a pattern going, or if I’m at home watching something, I might be focused on that for a while. But “so absorbed” that I don’t know what’s happening around me… I don’t think that really happens much.\n\nI usually still have some awareness of people moving around or someone needing something. At work especially, you kind of have to keep one ear open because people might come through or ask for something, so I don’t think I disappear into a task like that. Maybe I zone in a little here and there, but I haven’t noticed myself forgetting everything else around me.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse ever caused problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I’d have to say yes, at least at work. I don’t love saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m just careless or trying to be difficult, but it has started causing problems.\n\nThe main thing is when we’re turning rooms over or when there are supplies to decide about — like what needs restocking first, what can wait, which rooms need to be done in what order. If instructions come at me quickly, or if there are a couple people talking, I’ll catch part of it and then move on like I understood the whole thing. Then later someone says, “No, that’s not what I told you,” or “Why did you do that first?” And I realize I missed a piece of what they said.\n\nIt can also create friction with coworkers, because I might jump in with what I think needs to happen or make a quick call about supplies before everyone’s agreed on it. In my mind I’m trying to keep things moving, but it comes across like I’m not listening or I’m pushing ahead. I’ve been corrected for it more than once.\n\nMy supervisor has complained about it too, not just casually. It got to the point where he suggested — or kind of pushed me toward — using the employee assistance program. That was embarrassing, honestly. I don’t think I’m causing huge disasters or anything, but it’s enough that people are noticing and saying something. At home or relationships, I’m less sure how to measure it, but at work, yeah, it has caused real problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting, and acting on impulse consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been the same all through my adult life, no. I mean, I’ve probably always been the kind of person who likes to keep moving and maybe talks or jumps in a little fast sometimes, but the part where it’s really causing problems — where people are correcting me and I’m making quick decisions before I’ve fully heard everything — that’s been more recent.\n\nIt’s been, let me think… about a year and five months now, roughly. It really started getting worse after my cleaning contract changed. Before that, the work was more steady and predictable, and I could kind of get into a routine. But once it switched to rotating hours and more event work, everything got more rushed and changeable. Different timing, different rooms, different priorities depending on what was going on that day.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had this exact level of trouble consistently my whole adult life. This worse stretch has been going on for about a year and five months, since that contract change.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse since childhood and continued to have them as an adult?\n\nPatient: Yeah… when you put it that way, I guess yes, there was some of that going back pretty far. I don’t know that I would’ve described it the same way when I was a kid, but I do remember in grade school getting in trouble for not staying in my seat. Like I’d pop up when I wasn’t supposed to, or start moving around before the teacher was done talking.\n\nAnd talking over people — yeah, I got corrected for that too. I can remember teachers saying I needed to wait my turn, or that I was jumping in before they finished giving directions. I’d think I knew what they were going to say and just rush ahead, and then sometimes I’d miss the rest of the instructions.\n\nMy mother used to say I was like I was “wound too tight,” even as a kid. That’s the phrase I remember. Not in a mean way exactly, but like I had this motor in me and she couldn’t get me to settle down. So yes, there’s a childhood version of it. I think the work problems now are more noticeable because there are consequences, but the pattern of being too quick, too restless, interrupting — that wasn’t brand new.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse consistently since childhood into your adult life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it’s not like I had the words for it then, but I remember pretty clearly getting in trouble back in grade school for not staying put. I’d pop out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d be halfway up before the teacher was even done explaining what we were supposed to do.\n\nAnd I did talk over the teacher sometimes. Not trying to be disrespectful, at least not how I remember it, but I’d think I knew where they were going with something and I’d jump in or start doing the assignment before the directions were finished. Then, of course, I’d miss some important part and have to be corrected.\n\nMy mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” even when I was a kid. That phrase stuck with me because she said it more than once — like I had too much motor in me and couldn’t settle myself down. So, yeah, I’d say it’s been there since childhood in some form, even if the way it shows up now as an adult is a little different.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life, beyond just the issues at work?\n\nPatient: Well… the biggest problems I can point to are still at work, if I’m being honest. That’s where it shows up most clearly and where people actually say something to me about it.\n\nIt’s been causing complaints and corrections, not just little annoyances. Like during room turnovers, when everything has to move fast, I’ll jump in before I’ve heard the whole instruction, or I’ll think I know what needs doing and start moving supplies or making a decision about what goes where. Then it turns out I missed part of what was said, or someone else had already planned it differently, and that creates friction. People get irritated because they feel like I’m not listening or I’m pushing ahead, and I can see why it comes across that way.\n\nThe supply decisions are another one. I’ll act too quickly — like decide what needs to be brought up or used without fully checking or waiting — and then my supervisor has to correct me. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be difficult. I’m trying to be useful and keep things moving, but I end up making it harder sometimes.\n\nIt got to the point where my supervisor actually pushed me toward the employee assistance program, which felt… pretty serious to me. So yes, it’s causing problems in daily life in the sense that it’s not just something I notice inside my own head. Other people are reacting to it, correcting me, complaining, and it’s affecting how I get through the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes — definitely more than six months. The worse stretch, the part where it’s been really noticeable and causing problems, has been going on for about a year and five months now.\n\nIt really seemed to kick up after my cleaning contract changed. My hours started rotating more, and there was more event work involved, so the routine wasn’t as steady as it used to be. Since then, the sitting still, jumping in too fast, interrupting or acting before I’ve fully taken things in — that’s been pretty consistent. It’s not like it was just a bad week or a short period. It’s been over a year, closer to a year and a half.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties consistently over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yes, over the past year it’s been pretty consistent. I’d actually put it a little longer than that — about a year and five months, give or take.\n\nIt seemed to really get worse after my cleaning contract changed. Before that, things were more predictable, but once it shifted to rotating hours and more event work, it just felt like I was constantly having to adjust on the fly. Since then, these problems have been showing up regularly, not just here and there. It hasn’t felt like a short rough patch that passed. It’s been ongoing enough that I can look back and say, yeah, this has been the pattern for well over a year now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F90.8", "F90.9"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I’ll shift around in the chair, tap my feet, stand up if I can get away with it, or I’ll find some excuse like needing to check something or grab water just so I can move around a little. I remember getting in trouble for popping up out of my seat when we were supposed to be doing quiet work, or blurting things out and talking over the teacher before I even realized I was doing it. I’ll pace back and forth, keep hitting the button even though I know that doesn’t help, or start trying to figure out some other way to get moving before it’s really time.\n\nSame thing with the supply closet key. I don’t think of myself as someone who’s always late or totally unable to plan, but I guess it depends what you mean. If the day gets changed around or someone asks me to do things in a different order, then I might have to stop and figure out what’s most important first. But I’m not sure I’d say organizing tasks is my main problem.\n\nAt home, too, I sort of do things as they come up. I don’t really sit down and make a big plan, but I get by. So I guess my answer is maybe sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as a major issue the way some other things are.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I don’t really think of myself that way, like getting so locked into something that I forget the whole world is there.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I’m doing a task, especially something repetitive, I can kind of get into the rhythm of it — like cleaning a stretch of floor or working through a set of rooms — but I wouldn’t say I lose track of everything around me. I still usually notice if somebody comes by or if I’m needed somewhere else. At least I think I do.\n\nMaybe if I’m watching something at home or looking at something on my phone, I might tune out a little, but I don’t know if that’s more than what most people do. Like at work, if I’m waiting on the freight elevator with my cart, and it’s taking forever, I get really antsy. I’ll pace back and forth, keep hitting the button even though I know that doesn’t help, or start trying to figure out some other way to get moving before it’s really time.\n\nSame thing with the supply closet key. But as far as “careless mistakes,” I guess I’d have to think about it more. Nobody’s really sat me down and said that’s a big problem with me, at least not that I can remember. So… I don’t know. I don’t really think of myself that way, like getting so locked into something that I forget the whole world is there.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I’m doing a task, especially something repetitive, I can kind of get into the rhythm of it — like cleaning a stretch of floor or working through a set of rooms — but I wouldn’t say I lose track of everything around me. I still usually notice if somebody comes by or if I’m needed somewhere else. Nobody’s really told me, “You were impossible to get through to,” or anything like that. I don’t love saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m just careless or trying to be difficult, but it has started causing problems.\n\nThe main thing is when we’re turning rooms over or when there are supplies to decide about — like what needs restocking first, what can wait, which rooms need to be done in what order. In my mind I’m trying to keep things moving, but it comes across like I’m not listening or I’m pushing ahead. I’ve been corrected for it more than once.\n\nMy supervisor has complained about it too, not just casually. That phrase stuck with me because she said it more than once — like I had too much motor in me and couldn’t settle myself down. Yes, I think so. I mean, not in the exact same way every single day of my life, but when I think back, it does go way back to when I was a kid.

In grade school I remember getting in trouble for popping up out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to. Like I’d just be up before I even realized I was doing it. And I’d talk over the teacher sometimes, or answer before she was even done explaining the question. I also remember rushing ahead on things before the directions were finished, and then I’d have to be corrected because I hadn’t waited long enough to hear the whole instruction.

My mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” even as a kid. That phrase sticks in my head because she said it more than once — like I had this motor in me and couldn’t just settle. So yes, looking back, this isn’t something that came out of nowhere recently. The work situation made it more obvious lately, but that kind of restlessness and jumping in too fast has been there since childhood.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess the main thing is I can’t seem to sit still when I’m supposed to. It’s like there’s this motor humming under my skin, if that makes sense. Like in staff meetings at work, or sometimes at church when they’re doing event briefings and everybody’s sitting there listening, I’m trying to stay put but my body just wants to move. I’ll shift around in the chair, tap my feet, stand up if I can get away with it, or I’ll find some excuse like needing to check something or grab water just so I can move around a little. It’s uncomfortable, like I’m crawling out of my own skin when I have to just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, thinking back, that was kind of me all through grade school. I remember getting in trouble for popping up out of my seat when we were supposed to be doing quiet work, or blurting things out and talking over the teacher before I even realized I was doing it. And if the teacher was giving directions, I’d be halfway into the assignment before she was done explaining, then I’d have to be corrected because I’d missed something important.\n\nMy mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” like I had too much energy packed into me. At the time I just thought I was being a normal kid, but looking back, yeah, sitting still was a real problem for me even then.\n\nDoctor: Did you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finished speaking when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did, and honestly I still do that now. Back in school I would jump in before the teacher finished the question, like my mouth would just go ahead of me. I’d think I knew where they were going, so I’d answer too fast, and sometimes I was wrong because I hadn’t let them finish.\n\nThat still happens with my supervisor or with people at church. If they start giving me instructions, I’ll cut in with, “Oh, so you want me to do this,” before they’ve actually finished saying it. And then sometimes they’ll have to stop me and say, “No, that’s not what I meant,” or correct me because I guessed wrong. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be rude. It just comes out before I’ve really had a chance to hold it back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like doing things on the spur of the moment, outside of just speaking or interrupting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so, though for me it shows up a lot as not being able to wait my turn, if that counts as acting too fast. Like at work, if I’m waiting on the freight elevator with my cart, and it’s taking forever, I get really antsy. I’ll pace back and forth, keep hitting the button even though I know that doesn’t help, or start trying to figure out some other way to get moving before it’s really time.\n\nSame thing with the supply closet key. If somebody else has it, I have a hard time just standing there calmly waiting. I’ll be asking, “Are you almost done with that?” or hovering around, and I can tell I’m getting short with people even when they’re not doing anything wrong. Or if we’re waiting for someone to decide which room needs cleaning next, I’m already wanting to jump in and start somewhere just so I’m not stuck waiting.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to bulldoze people, but that waiting feeling makes me feel wound up and impatient, and I push to get things moving before it’s really my turn.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure on that one. I mean, I know I make mistakes like anyone does, especially if I’m tired or there’s a lot going on, but I don’t know that I’d say I’m constantly missing little details in my work.\n\nWith cleaning, a lot of it is pretty routine, so I can usually see if something’s obviously not done right — like if a trash can wasn’t emptied or a floor still looks dirty. But as far as “careless mistakes,” I guess I’d have to think about it more. Nobody’s really sat me down and said that’s a big problem with me, at least not that I can remember. So… I don’t know. That’s not the first thing that comes to mind for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I do have routines at work, so in that sense the job kind of organizes me. There are certain areas that have to be cleaned, trash has to go out, supplies have to be checked, that kind of thing. If I’m following the usual routine, I can usually get through it.\n\nTime management… I don’t know. I don’t think of myself as someone who’s always late or totally unable to plan, but I guess it depends what you mean. If the day gets changed around or someone asks me to do things in a different order, then I might have to stop and figure out what’s most important first. But I’m not sure I’d say organizing tasks is my main problem.\n\nAt home, too, I sort of do things as they come up. I don’t really sit down and make a big plan, but I get by. So I guess my answer is maybe sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as a major issue the way some other things are.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I don’t really think of myself that way, like getting so locked into something that I forget the whole world is there.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I’m doing a task, especially something repetitive, I can kind of get into the rhythm of it — like cleaning a stretch of floor or working through a set of rooms — but I wouldn’t say I lose track of everything around me. I still usually notice if somebody comes by or if I’m needed somewhere else. At least I think I do.\n\nMaybe if I’m watching something at home or looking at something on my phone, I might tune out a little, but I don’t know if that’s more than what most people do. Nobody’s really told me, “You were impossible to get through to,” or anything like that. So I guess my answer is… not really, or not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so absorbed in an activity that you forget about other things that are happening around you?\n\nPatient: I guess I’d say I’m not really sure, but not in a way that stands out to me. Like, I can get into doing something, sure — if I’m cleaning an area and I’ve got a pattern going, or if I’m at home watching something, I might be focused on that for a while. But “so absorbed” that I don’t know what’s happening around me… I don’t think that really happens much.\n\nI usually still have some awareness of people moving around or someone needing something. At work especially, you kind of have to keep one ear open because people might come through or ask for something, so I don’t think I disappear into a task like that. Maybe I zone in a little here and there, but I haven’t noticed myself forgetting everything else around me.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse ever caused problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I’d have to say yes, at least at work. I don’t love saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m just careless or trying to be difficult, but it has started causing problems.\n\nThe main thing is when we’re turning rooms over or when there are supplies to decide about — like what needs restocking first, what can wait, which rooms need to be done in what order. If instructions come at me quickly, or if there are a couple people talking, I’ll catch part of it and then move on like I understood the whole thing. Then later someone says, “No, that’s not what I told you,” or “Why did you do that first?” And I realize I missed a piece of what they said.\n\nIt can also create friction with coworkers, because I might jump in with what I think needs to happen or make a quick call about supplies before everyone’s agreed on it. In my mind I’m trying to keep things moving, but it comes across like I’m not listening or I’m pushing ahead. I’ve been corrected for it more than once.\n\nMy supervisor has complained about it too, not just casually. It got to the point where he suggested — or kind of pushed me toward — using the employee assistance program. That was embarrassing, honestly. I don’t think I’m causing huge disasters or anything, but it’s enough that people are noticing and saying something. At home or relationships, I’m less sure how to measure it, but at work, yeah, it has caused real problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting, and acting on impulse consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been the same all through my adult life, no. I mean, I’ve probably always been the kind of person who likes to keep moving and maybe talks or jumps in a little fast sometimes, but the part where it’s really causing problems — where people are correcting me and I’m making quick decisions before I’ve fully heard everything — that’s been more recent.\n\nIt’s been, let me think… about a year and five months now, roughly. It really started getting worse after my cleaning contract changed. Before that, the work was more steady and predictable, and I could kind of get into a routine. But once it switched to rotating hours and more event work, everything got more rushed and changeable. Different timing, different rooms, different priorities depending on what was going on that day.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had this exact level of trouble consistently my whole adult life. This worse stretch has been going on for about a year and five months, since that contract change.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse since childhood and continued to have them as an adult?\n\nPatient: Yeah… when you put it that way, I guess yes, there was some of that going back pretty far. I don’t know that I would’ve described it the same way when I was a kid, but I do remember in grade school getting in trouble for not staying in my seat. Like I’d pop up when I wasn’t supposed to, or start moving around before the teacher was done talking.\n\nAnd talking over people — yeah, I got corrected for that too. I can remember teachers saying I needed to wait my turn, or that I was jumping in before they finished giving directions. I’d think I knew what they were going to say and just rush ahead, and then sometimes I’d miss the rest of the instructions.\n\nMy mother used to say I was like I was “wound too tight,” even as a kid. That’s the phrase I remember. Not in a mean way exactly, but like I had this motor in me and she couldn’t get me to settle down. So yes, there’s a childhood version of it. I think the work problems now are more noticeable because there are consequences, but the pattern of being too quick, too restless, interrupting — that wasn’t brand new.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse consistently since childhood into your adult life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it’s not like I had the words for it then, but I remember pretty clearly getting in trouble back in grade school for not staying put. I’d pop out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d be halfway up before the teacher was even done explaining what we were supposed to do.\n\nAnd I did talk over the teacher sometimes. Not trying to be disrespectful, at least not how I remember it, but I’d think I knew where they were going with something and I’d jump in or start doing the assignment before the directions were finished. Then, of course, I’d miss some important part and have to be corrected.\n\nMy mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” even when I was a kid. That phrase stuck with me because she said it more than once — like I had too much motor in me and couldn’t settle myself down. So, yeah, I’d say it’s been there since childhood in some form, even if the way it shows up now as an adult is a little different.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life, beyond just the issues at work?\n\nPatient: Well… the biggest problems I can point to are still at work, if I’m being honest. That’s where it shows up most clearly and where people actually say something to me about it.\n\nIt’s been causing complaints and corrections, not just little annoyances. Like during room turnovers, when everything has to move fast, I’ll jump in before I’ve heard the whole instruction, or I’ll think I know what needs doing and start moving supplies or making a decision about what goes where. Then it turns out I missed part of what was said, or someone else had already planned it differently, and that creates friction. People get irritated because they feel like I’m not listening or I’m pushing ahead, and I can see why it comes across that way.\n\nThe supply decisions are another one. I’ll act too quickly — like decide what needs to be brought up or used without fully checking or waiting — and then my supervisor has to correct me. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be difficult. I’m trying to be useful and keep things moving, but I end up making it harder sometimes.\n\nIt got to the point where my supervisor actually pushed me toward the employee assistance program, which felt… pretty serious to me. So yes, it’s causing problems in daily life in the sense that it’s not just something I notice inside my own head. Other people are reacting to it, correcting me, complaining, and it’s affecting how I get through the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes — definitely more than six months. The worse stretch, the part where it’s been really noticeable and causing problems, has been going on for about a year and five months now.\n\nIt really seemed to kick up after my cleaning contract changed. My hours started rotating more, and there was more event work involved, so the routine wasn’t as steady as it used to be. Since then, the sitting still, jumping in too fast, interrupting or acting before I’ve fully taken things in — that’s been pretty consistent. It’s not like it was just a bad week or a short period. It’s been over a year, closer to a year and a half.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties consistently over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yes, over the past year it’s been pretty consistent. I’d actually put it a little longer than that — about a year and five months, give or take.\n\nIt seemed to really get worse after my cleaning contract changed. Before that, things were more predictable, but once it shifted to rotating hours and more event work, it just felt like I was constantly having to adjust on the fly. Since then, these problems have been showing up regularly, not just here and there. It hasn’t felt like a short rough patch that passed. It’s been ongoing enough that I can look back and say, yeah, this has been the pattern for well over a year now.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties affected your relationships with family or friends outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I can’t point to something clear the way I can with work, where someone has actually complained or corrected me.\n\nWith family or friends… I guess I’d have to think about it more. Nobody’s sat me down and said, “You’re causing problems,” or anything like that. I don’t want to say it hasn’t affected anything at all, because sometimes people close to you just adapt or they don’t say it directly, but I haven’t noticed a big relationship fallout or a specific friendship getting damaged because of it.\n\nSo, no — not in any obvious way that I can clearly identify. If it has affected them, it’s been more subtle than what’s happening at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, or acting on impulse continuously since your childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, not in the exact same way every single day of my life, but when I think back, it does go way back to when I was a kid.\n\nIn grade school I remember getting in trouble for popping up out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to. Like I’d just be up before I even realized I was doing it. And I’d talk over the teacher sometimes, or answer before she was even done explaining the question. I also remember rushing ahead on things before the directions were finished, and then I’d have to be corrected because I hadn’t waited long enough to hear the whole instruction.\n\nMy mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” even as a kid. That phrase sticks in my head because she said it more than once — like I had this motor in me and couldn’t just settle. So yes, looking back, this isn’t something that came out of nowhere recently. The work situation made it more obvious lately, but that kind of restlessness and jumping in too fast has been there since childhood.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I’d say it’s definitely been causing real problems for more than six months.

The clearest place is work. It’s not just that I feel restless or annoyed inside — it’s started turning into actual complaints and corrections from other people. Like, I’ll miss part of what someone told me because I jump ahead or I think I already understand the instruction, and then I end up doing it not quite the way they wanted. During room turnovers, especially when things are moving fast, that can create friction because people are expecting me to follow the plan exactly and I’m already acting on what I thought needed doing.

And with supplies, too — decisions about what to restock or what needs to be moved where — I’ve had situations where I act too quickly or don’t wait for the full direction, and then someone has to come back and correct me. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m not trying to be difficult. I’m trying to keep things moving, but it comes off like I’m not listening or I’m pushing my own way.

It got to the point where my supervisor suggested I contact the employee assistance program. That was kind of a wake-up call for me, because that made it feel less like, “Oh, I’m just having a rough week,” and more like, “Okay, this is affecting the job enough that management is noticing.” So yes, I’d say it’s been significant, and it’s been going on long enough that I can’t really brush it off as temporary.

Like at work, if I’m waiting on the freight elevator with my cart, and it’s taking forever, I get really antsy. I’ll pace back and forth, keep hitting the button even though I know that doesn’t help, or start trying to figure out some other way to get moving before it’s really time.\n\nSame thing with the supply closet key. If somebody else has it, I have a hard time just standing there calmly waiting. I’ll be asking, “Are you almost done with that?” or hovering around, and I can tell I’m getting short with people even when they’re not doing anything wrong. I mean, I know I make mistakes like anyone does, especially if I’m tired or there’s a lot going on, but I don’t know that I’d say I’m constantly missing little details in my work.\n\nWith cleaning, a lot of it is pretty routine, so I can usually see if something’s obviously not done right — like if a trash can wasn’t emptied or a floor still looks dirty. But as far as “careless mistakes,” I guess I’d have to think about it more. Nobody’s really sat me down and said that’s a big problem with me, at least not that I can remember. So… I don’t know. I mean, I do have routines at work, so in that sense the job kind of organizes me. There are certain areas that have to be cleaned, trash has to go out, supplies have to be checked, that kind of thing. I don’t think of myself as someone who’s always late or totally unable to plan, but I guess it depends what you mean. If the day gets changed around or someone asks me to do things in a different order, then I might have to stop and figure out what’s most important first. So I guess my answer is maybe sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as a major issue the way some other things are.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I don’t really think of myself that way, like getting so locked into something that I forget the whole world is there.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I’m doing a task, especially something repetitive, I can kind of get into the rhythm of it — like cleaning a stretch of floor or working through a set of rooms — but I wouldn’t say I lose track of everything around me. I still usually notice if somebody comes by or if I’m needed somewhere else. So I guess my answer is… not really, or not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so absorbed in an activity that you forget about other things that are happening around you?\n\nPatient: I guess I’d say I’m not really sure, but not in a way that stands out to me. Like, I can get into doing something, sure — if I’m cleaning an area and I’ve got a pattern going, or if I’m at home watching something, I might be focused on that for a while. I don’t love saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m just careless or trying to be difficult, but it has started causing problems.\n\nThe main thing is when we’re turning rooms over or when there are supplies to decide about — like what needs restocking first, what can wait, which rooms need to be done in what order. If instructions come at me quickly, or if there are a couple people talking, I’ll catch part of it and then move on like I understood the whole thing. I don’t know that I would’ve described it the same way when I was a kid, but I do remember in grade school getting in trouble for not staying in my seat. Like I’d pop up when I wasn’t supposed to, or start moving around before the teacher was done talking.\n\nAnd talking over people — yeah, I got corrected for that too. I can remember teachers saying I needed to wait my turn, or that I was jumping in before they finished giving directions.
